### 5.9 Team structure, rebuild and sustainability

- The team lost 50% of its members — its experienced leaders — to graduation, and rebuilt the
  leadership pipeline within one season, growing from 7 to 14 members
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-3).
- Constraints the team names: annual senior graduation, rookie skill gaps, limited build-season
  time, risk of knowledge loss, no team mentors, and a 50% mid-season budget cut
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-c.png;
  content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg).
- The sustainability strategy is the **"Hydra" model**: instead of one expert per role, the team
  cross-trains across subteams, keeps leadership redundancy, integrates rookies early, documents
  transitions, distributes subsystem ownership and runs monthly structured development
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-c.png;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-3).
- Season goals: increase female participation, cross-train, expand outreach, improve documentation
  and tracking. Subteam process: rotate between teams, select based on interest, 1:1 mentoring, own
  a project (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-e.jpg).
- After four female leads graduated, the team recruited a rookie class that was **67% female**
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-e.jpg;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-3).
- Measured improvements over the season: subsystem testing cycles 5% → 90%; component iteration
  cycles 2 → 5+; engineering task distribution 3 → 8 active contributors; build access hours
  4.5 → 8 per week; rookies assumed subsystem ownership in week 3
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-3).
- Three teammates completed a summer STEM internship; the team lists experience in advanced
  calculus, physics, materials science, structured documentation, accountability systems and
  engineering workflows (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg).
- Sustain cycle: a 12-month loop — April–May transition and reflection, June–August rookie skill
  expansion, September–November structured engineering sprints, December–February subsystem
  ownership, March succession and documentation — containing 6 structured technical training
  stages, 4 major sprint reviews, 3 qualifier feedback loops, 1 formal leadership transition plan,
  and 100% of roles documented (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-17).
- Documentation protocols were established in August, the portfolio is updated after each
  qualifier, and lessons learned are archived in March
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-17).
- Tooling: Discord for team communications and the FTC Discord; Onshape for CAD collaboration;
  Canvas Gantt charts to create and track workflow, outreach, feedback and expert impact
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-3).
- Budget after the 50% cut, as itemized: Electronics $611; Hardware $790.11; Materials $803.66;
  Fees $494.71; Limelight $189; Team Shirts $157.50; FLL Shirts $105; printed TOTAL $3,121
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-3).
- The team answered the budget cut by launching an FTC MicroFunding website
  (https://microfunding.tritonics.org/), implementing budget tracking, and using sustainable
  materials (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg;
  content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-b.png).
- The micro-funding platform is student-designed; it lets robotics teams create campaigns and
  receive community support to fund programs, start teams, or contribute to a shared robotics fund,
  and supports both FTC and FRC teams
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-5;
  content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-b.png).
- On the roster, the micro-funding website is credited to Milind A (Code Specialist, Y2), not to
  Alex (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-e.jpg).
- Alex's private drafts record about $2,000 raised to send a Ukrainian FTC team to the World
  Championship (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L4378-L4379), and the older résumé
  states $1,000 raised for a Ukraine robotics team (content/source/resume/resume-older.png).
- TODO(alex): The budget lines sum to $3,150.98 but the printed total is $3,121
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg). Resolve before quoting either.
- TODO(alex): Root CLAUDE.md credits the micro-funding platform with helping robotics teams in

  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-8). Confirm what Alex built or led here

### 5.10 Training and mentoring counts

- **50+ students trained** in robotics (CLAUDE.md; corroborated in Alex's private drafts at
  content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1523-L1524).
- The team's Reach page reports **50+ new students introduced to FIRST values** and **10+ who
  completed tech training**, through twice-weekly fabrication classes, DECODE-themed STEM
  workshops, Hour of Code and a rookie training program
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png).
- Rookie training is stage-based and 1:1, covering Java, Fusion, safety, tools, machining and
  presentation; every rookie completes a functional subsystem
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-17;
  content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-c.png).
- Each rookie also plans an outreach event with mentor feedback at every stage
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-c.png).
- The two-week Tritonics Summer FTC Camp is the primary recruitment engine — build challenges,
  coding workshops, mini-competitions. It produced **6 new members** for Tritonics and **15
  students recruited to form a new FTC team**, which qualified for the city championship
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-b.png;
  content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-c.png).
- **75+ hours** were spent recruiting (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-a.png;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-3).
- The team hosted **2 robotics mini-competitions** and launched and mentored **1 additional FTC
  team** (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg).
- Team development stages are Gather Seeds → Plant & Nurture → Grow Roots → Blossom, with reported
  impact of 100% of members learning new skills, producing prototypes, designing and planning
  outreach, and reporting more confidence; 5 FLL teams and 7 FTC teams funded and/or mentored; and
  300+ students who tried robotics and coding
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-c.png).
- Alex's roster card names team-building — recruiting and training rookies — as what he is proudest
  of (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-e.jpg).
- Alex's private drafts additionally record mentoring 12 FLL/FTC teams and designing STEM curricula
  for 60+ students across NYC, NJ and Montana reservation schools
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1574-L1577).
- Distinct from robotics training: the IDEA Club, which Alex co-founded, trained **70+ students**
  in 3D CAD/printing, design thinking, rapid prototyping, fabrication and shop safety
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png; CLAUDE.md). See §7 of this section set — do not merge
  the 70+ IDEA figure with the 50+ robotics figure.

  the same population as the team's "50+ new students to FIRST values"
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png).

### 5.11 Connect: the expert network

- The team's Connect task was to build a regenerative network that expands team skills, leadership
  and technical capabilities, under constraints of being student-led, time, cold outreach, and
  skill gaps emerging through the season
  (content/source/robotics/tritonics-portfolio/tritonics-portfolio.png#P5).
- The expert network is named **"Hydra"**; students research experts and connect via LinkedIn or
  email, using templates and tracked follow-up to grow each contact into an ongoing mentor
  (content/source/robotics/tritonics-portfolio/tritonics-portfolio.png#P5).
- Five-step process: identify skill gaps with a SWOT chart; calendar priorities; identify experts
  inside and outside the community; connect and meet; follow up and start a feedback loop
  (content/source/robotics/tritonics-portfolio/tritonics-portfolio.png#P5).
- **38 experts** connected with, and **150+ hours** of expert engagement; **71%** were multi-session
  or ongoing connections and **28%** generated secondary connections
  (content/source/robotics/tritonics-portfolio/tritonics-portfolio.png#P5;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-7).
- Experts by area: 10% game strategy and performance; 16% software and controls; 18% fabrication and
  manufacturing; 26% hardware and reliability; 30% leadership, ops and communications
  (content/source/robotics/tritonics-portfolio/tritonics-portfolio.png#P5).
- The poster deck's headcount version: Hardware & Reliability 9; Fabrication & Manufacturing 7;
  Software & Controls 6; Game Strategy 4; Leadership Development 4; Operations & Planning 3;
  Communications & Branding 3; and it also reports 26 technical plus 10 organizational experts,
  100% student-initiated, with 10+ secondary introductions
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-7).
- Technical results the team attributes to these connections: a custom pathfinding algorithm,
  refined launch force modeling, sensor-based launcher speed control, CNC tolerance improvements and
  reduced mechanical failure (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-7).
- Organizations shown in the expert-network logo strip include NASA, Design that Matters, Duke MEMS,
  TIME, Georgia Tech FIRST Robotics, Michigan Robotics, USC Iovine and Young Academy and NYU
  (content/source/robotics/tritonics-portfolio/tritonics-portfolio.png#P5).
- Four Connect projects are documented on the championship slide, each run by a team lead: a
  **Modular Prosthetic Arm** with Professor Long Wang (Infinite Technologies and Stevens logos); an
  **Educational Fusor Reactor**; **Autonomous Path Optimization** with a Simons Foundation applied
  mathematician; and **Golden Record Space Outreach** with Microsoft R&D engineers and the original
  Golden Record designer (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-a.png).
- The Connect page states that knowledge from these collaborations directly influenced the robot's
  modular design and autonomous pathing algorithm
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-a.png).
- The Modular Prosthetic Arm project matches Alex's Stevens research work on the résumé — designing
  and fabricating a prosthetic arm under faculty supervision, summer 2024
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png) — and his private drafts name Professor Long Wang as
  the Stevens mentor (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L2513). The slide itself
  names no student (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-a.png).

### 5.12 Reach: outreach and the FIRST pipeline

- Objectives: expand access to FIRST in underserved communities, build sustained robotics
  pipelines, recruit new teams/coaches/mentors/volunteers, model Gracious Professionalism and
  Coopertition, and convert exposure to participation to leadership. The strategy is 100%
  student-led outreach with 100% teammate participation
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png).
- Headline totals: **500+ outreach hours**; **10,000+ individuals reached**; **12+ FIRST teams
  mentored or created**; **5 FLL teams mentored weekly**; **1 FTC team created**; **2 sustained
  international partnerships**; **80+ schools provided curriculum**; **28 new FIRST mentors**
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-b.png).
- The poster deck's version adds **~35+ unique organizations** engaged, **60%+ multi-session**
  engagements, and outreach initiated 76% by current students and 24% by alumni
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-8).
- Recruitment impact: 1 new team, 4 new coaches, 24 new mentors, 18 new volunteers
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-b.png).
- FIRST pipeline programs: 5 FLL teams mentored weekly; 1 FTC team launched and NYC
  Championship-qualified; 12 FIRST teams launched or supported; **750 students reached with a K–8
  robotics curriculum**; and from Hour of Code sessions, **250+ students introduced to FIRST
  concepts, 80+ who transitioned into FIRST programs, and 60+ who later joined FTC or FLL teams**
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-5).
- Hour of Code is listed at **30+ sessions**, K–8
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-b.png).
- Sustained-engagement partners: 5 FLL teams weekly; Ghana FTC monthly; Blackfoot Reserve FLL teams
  monthly plus fundraising; Team Tic-Tac Tech #32706; Bosworth FTC #21377; Hour of Code (30+
  sessions); All Saints NJ monthly; a senior center, ongoing; FDAVII STEM Lab weekly
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png).
- Hudson Guild appears in the team documents only as a named nonprofit partner, with no program
  detail (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-8;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-5).
- Alex's own portfolio page is the source for the Hudson Guild programs: he ran weekly programs
  introducing younger students to robotics and other basic STEM concepts
  (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1).
- His portfolio page also records organizing and running over **20 Hour of Code workshops** and
  designing a curriculum for lower-grade students on coding and logical thinking, and an in-school
  e-waste drive that collected **40+ pounds** of e-waste, donated or properly recycled
  (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1).
- At the time that page was made he was working to expand the e-waste drive city-wide, with dozens
  of collection points and promotion, with help from the NYC Department of Sanitation
  (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1).
- This season the team helped launch FIRST programs where none previously existed: a Montana
  reservation community, Accra (Ghana), and local underserved NJ communities, working with educators
  to train coaches, support team formation and fundraising, provide ongoing mentorship and share
  curriculum (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-b.png).
- In Ghana the team ran monthly FTC workshops at Accra High School
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-29).
- Other programs in the deck: teaching bike-repair mechanical engineering skills to 20+ kids at
  FreeCycles in Missoula, Montana, and collaborating with Blackfoot 8th graders to introduce
  robotics, STEM opportunities and scholarships
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-29).
- A student-led **FTC Parts Drive** collected spare FTC components, which were delivered to schools
  and teams at the Super Qualifier (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-b.png;
  content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-b.png).
- **DECODE Engineering Modules** are student-created, game-based STEM outreach — mini intake design
  challenges, rapid-fire transfer simulations, strategy optimization exercises — used in rookie
  training, Hour of Code, the summer camp and community events; **94% of participating students
  said they were interested in exploring FIRST**. A laser-cut wooden cipher "Decoder" wheel was made
  for it (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png).
- The team reached out to the Hoboken Mayor for political support, and reports the awareness effort
  reaching over 10,000 people (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-b.png).
- Over **30 total outreach events** were run, and each team member designed and led one outreach
  initiative (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-3).
- Root CLAUDE.md additionally credits Alex with CAD tutorials in seven languages (CLAUDE.md);
  his private drafts corroborate seven languages and tie the tutorials to collaboration with
  under-resourced public-school teams at Robot-in-2-Days
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L4098-L4101). No team document mentions them.

## 6. Featured: CeraPiper

**Role:** Research Intern. The résumé entry reads "Cera Piper (Cornell Tech x Technion) — Research
Intern" and states that he worked with Cornell Tech and Technion to develop CAD and slicing software
for a custom ceramic printer. No CeraPiper source document names Alex or states which parts of the
system he built — see the authorship subsection and the TODO(alex) lines below.
(content/source/resume/resume-current.png; CLAUDE.md — Featured work)

**Dates:** Summer 2026. (content/source/resume/resume-current.png; CLAUDE.md — Featured work)

**One line:** CeraPiper is a CAD tool and fabrication system for designing and extruding custom
hollow ceramic pipes that assemble into structures which cool a space by evaporating water.
(content/source/cerapiper/README.md#about-the-project)

**Name to publish:** CeraPiper, one word, as the project's own README spells it. The résumé writes it
"Cera Piper"; the résumé also misspells Cornell as "Conell" in the body text.
(content/source/cerapiper/README.md#about-the-project; content/source/resume/resume-current.png)

---

### 6.1 Role, affiliation, and authorship — exactly what the documents say

**What the résumé says**

- The entry is "Cera Piper (Cornell Tech x Technion) — Research Intern, Summer 2026"
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).
- The résumé describes the work as developing CAD and slicing software for a custom ceramic printer
  that prints pipes allowing sustainable active room cooling without electricity
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).
- The résumé's forward-looking line is that a research paper is eventually being written and approved
  by the SCF research conference. It does not claim a paper is published and does not name Alex as an
  author (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).

**What the project documents say**

- Neither `content/source/cerapiper/README.md` nor the paper draft contains the strings "Alex",
  "Tully", "Cornell", "Technion", or "intern" — verified by whole-file grep across both files
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md; content/source/cerapiper/user-study-paper/text.md).
- The README has no authors, contributors, or acknowledgments section; its only attribution is to the
  Matter of Tech Lab (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#contact).
- The paper draft's byline is "Anonymous Author(s)", because the draft is under anonymous review, so
  no author list exists in the file to check against
  (content/source/cerapiper/user-study-paper/text.md#page-1).
- The only human names anywhere in this source group are the authors of the earlier, published CAM
  paper: Ofer Berman, Ethan Zhi Ming Seiz, and Thijs Roumen. Alex is not among them
  (content/source/cerapiper/user-study-paper/text.md#page-16, reference [10]).
- The only institution named in the CeraPiper sources is the Matter of Tech Lab. The paper draft
  refers to the authors' university without naming it, and redacts the interview language as a
  placeholder for review (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#contact;
  content/source/cerapiper/user-study-paper/text.md#page-10, #page-18).
- The README describes the whole system as one piece of work and attributes no component to any
  individual (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).

**Consequence for the site.** Until Alex answers the TODOs in 6.10, the strongest role line the
sources support is the résumé's own: research intern, summer 2026, Cornell Tech x Technion, working
on the CAD and slicing software. The site must not claim authorship of either paper, and must not
attribute any specific layer of the system to Alex.
(content/source/resume/resume-current.png; content/source/cerapiper/README.md)

### 6.2 What the system is

**The problem it addresses**

- Evaporative cooling with ceramics is a low-cost, energy-efficient alternative to conventional air
  conditioning but has lacked accessible computational design tools; CeraPiper couples real-time
  control of extrusion geometry with a professional CAD environment
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#about-the-project).
- Users compose whole assemblies — pipes, ribs, curves, branches, and plug-and-socket connectors — as
  parametric features, and the tool translates the design into machine instructions for a
  shape-shifting die mounted on a commercial clay pug mill
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#about-the-project).
- The extruded segments assemble into modular cooling devices, described in the README as
  chandelier-style units, that passively cool their surroundings through water evaporation
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#about-the-project).

**Three software layers plus one physical artifact**

The README organizes the system as three layers, each named with its technology. That numbered stack
is the clearest structure for a technical section on the site.
(content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation)

**1. Design layer — Onshape, FeatureScript.**

- Custom parametric features are written in Onshape's FeatureScript: ribbed straight pipes, ribbed
  curved pipes, perpendicular branch pipes, plug-and-socket connectors, junction collars, and a
  linear pattern; all share a common library of fabrication constants
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
- All profiles are hexagonal, matching the die geometry
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
- Each feature encodes machine knowledge as one of three kinds of design-time constraint
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation):
  - **Hard constraints** restrict parameters to fabricable values: enumerated inner diameters (die
    mandrel sizes), outer diameters bounded to 40–78 mm, profile rotation limited to 30° increments
    that preserve hex alignment, and segment lengths capped at the 800 mm conveyor bed minus the
    fixed 60 mm start and end pieces (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
  - **Corrective constraints** repair invalid input instead of rejecting it: a ribbed pipe snaps its
    length to whole rib increments (rib width plus gap) and reports the adjustment, so extrusion
    never terminates mid-rib (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
  - **Advisory constraints** surface fabrication risk without blocking the design: an unsupported
    span beyond 150 mm triggers a sag warning and turns the geometry red in the viewport
    (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).

**2. Translation middleware — Python, Flask.**

- The middleware queries the Onshape feature tree through the REST API and reconstructs the assembly
  as a directed graph whose nodes are fabrication primitives and whose edges are connector
  dependencies (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
- Because ceramic extrusion is a continuous, sequential process, the middleware traverses the graph
  depth-first to linearize each branch into a contiguous extrusion sequence, splitting long runs at
  the conveyor-bed limit and splitting the assembly at perpendicular junctions that are completed by
  hand in post-processing (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
- The result is serialized into a JSON Function Stack — a procedural recipe of primitives and
  parameters — which the interface renders in a WebGL 3D preview and a 2D cross-section, and from
  which it generates a printable bed blueprint
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
- Bounding-box queries are requested only at topological intersections, to stay within Onshape API
  rate limits (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).

**3. Fabrication backend — CeraPiper, Arduino (C++).**

- The middleware transmits the compiled Function Stack over a USB serial link to an Arduino, whose
  firmware maps primitives to motor steps and actuator outputs, regulates the feed rate of the clay
  pug mill, and drives the shape-shifting die
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
- The firmware independently re-enforces mechanical thresholds, including the bed-length limit, as a
  safety layer, so violations are also caught at the machine
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).

**4. The printed paper blueprint (the physical layer).**

- CeraPiper prints a 20 cm-wide paper blueprint on a standard plotter
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#the-paper-blueprint).
- The sheet runs continuously along the conveyor bed beneath the freshly extruded clay, carrying
  localized design decisions alongside the material: which part is being extruded, where the clay is
  cut into fabricable segments, the mandrel size in use, and where holes must be hand-cut to receive
  perpendicular connectors (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#the-paper-blueprint).
- The blueprint has nine toggleable layers: grid and crop marks, part name/number, section outline,
  cut lines and connector cut-outs, fold guides and mandrel size, section highlights, notes, clay top
  view, and piece IDs — so a designer prints exactly the guidance they want on the shop floor
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#the-paper-blueprint).
- Each bed keeps its own part name, number, and notes
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#the-paper-blueprint).
- Some makers keep the sheet beside the extrusion; others extrude directly onto it
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#the-paper-blueprint).

**The interface**

- A tab bar splits the design into fabricable beds: Main, branches, and a Perpendicular Connectors
  bed (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation, cad-interface caption).
- The 3D viewport offers a straight view (pipes as extruded) and a curved view (simulated bending)
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
- A Components panel lists every primitive, with most parameters editable inline
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
- An interactive bed blueprint of the design is printed to guide extrusion and post-processing
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation).
- The editor loads a design straight from Onshape (paste a file URL or element ID, or enable "Load
  from Onshape API" in Settings) or from a saved `.json` design file
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#installation).

---

### 6.3 From CAM to CAD — the reformulation

- CeraPiper began as a machine-facing CAM interface: a standalone editor organized around the
  extrusion sequence the machine performs, where the designer sequenced geometric primitives along a
  single axis (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#from-cam-to-cad).
- The README's framing of why the CAD layer exists: that interface made the process "controllable,
  but not designable" — makers reasoned about extrusion parameters in isolation and could not compose
  or evaluate the assembled object. This is public README wording, not paper wording, and is the
  cleanest one-line explanation available for the site
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#from-cam-to-cad).
- The current tool recompiles that CAM workflow into a CAD one: each machine parameter is
  reformulated as an editable design element inside Onshape, so the assembled object — not the print
  sequence — becomes the primary representation
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#from-cam-to-cad).

The README's own two-column table is the most legible artifact in the source and translates directly
into a two-column block on the site. All values below are README values.
(content/source/cerapiper/README.md#from-cam-to-cad)

| The machine used to expose… | …now it's a design element |
|---|---|
| Die aperture travel | Outer diameter (40–78 mm), bounded to formable parts |
| Mandrel insert | Enumerated inner diameter (die mandrel sizes) |
| Extrusion duration | Length in millimeters, capped by the conveyor bed |
| Rib count, gap, width | Ribbed-pipe length that snaps to whole ribs |
| Rib spacing for bending | Curve radius and sweep angle |
| Instruction order | Derived automatically from how parts attach |
| Manual bed splitting | Automatic segmentation into fabricable tabs |
| Separate junction pieces | Branch feature with paired collars carrying carving marks |
| Duplicated stack entries | A single linear-pattern operation |
| Operator sag judgment | An advisory warning above a 150 mm unsupported span |

---

### 6.4 Numbers and constraints (all publishable, all from the README)

| Figure | What it is | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 40–78 mm | Outer diameter bound (hard constraint) | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation, #from-cam-to-cad) |
| 30° increments | Profile rotation limit, preserving hex alignment | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation) |
| 800 mm | Conveyor bed length that caps segment length | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation) |
| 60 mm | Fixed start and end pieces subtracted from the 800 mm bed | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation) |
| 150 mm | Maximum unsupported span before the sag advisory fires | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation, #from-cam-to-cad) |
| 20 cm | Width of the printed paper blueprint | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#the-paper-blueprint) |
| 9 | Toggleable blueprint layers | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#the-paper-blueprint) |
| 3 | Software layers (Onshape/FeatureScript, Python/Flask, Arduino C++) | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation) |
| 3 | Kinds of design-time constraint (hard, corrective, advisory) | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#implementation) |
| ~8–10% | Elevated relative humidity achieved by prototypes | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#about-the-project; external citation https://doi.org/10.1145/3745778.3766644) |
| 1.85 L / 60 hours | Water evaporated by prototypes | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#about-the-project; external citation https://doi.org/10.1145/3745778.3766644) |
| 1 API request | Typical Onshape requests per import after Version 2.1, down from one per part | (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#roadmap) |

- The README attributes the humidity and evaporation figures to technical evaluations of the
  underlying process, which also demonstrated repeatable geometric fidelity and measurable
  temperature reductions. Those evaluations belong to the earlier CAM work published at ACM SCF 2025,
  so the citation for both figures is that paper's DOI
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#about-the-project;
  https://doi.org/10.1145/3745778.3766644). See TODO(alex) in 6.10 — the exact numbers have not been
  checked against the published paper itself.
- The README gives no temperature-reduction number, only the word "measurable", so the site must not
  state one (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#about-the-project).

**Stack and dependencies** (useful for a skills line, all from the README)

- Prerequisites: Arduino Uno; Arduino IDE 2.3.3; ArduinoJson by Benoit Blanchon 7.4.2; Python 3;
  Flask 3.1.1; PySerial 3.5; plus Requests, NetworkX, python-dotenv, and Waitress for the Onshape
  integration (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#prerequisites).
- The app runs at `http://localhost:8000` (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#installation).
- All active application code lives under `development_copy/`; `app.py` is the Flask entry point and
  exposes `/`, `/settings`, `/extrude`, `/serial-log`, and `/api/generate-json`
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#file-structure).
- Backend modules: `api/main.py` (Onshape REST client, `normalize_feature_type`,
  `simulate_primitive`, `generate_extrusion_json`), `api/perpendicular_pipes.py` (branch expansion,
  host attachment, hole cut-outs), `api/matching.py` (map Onshape parts to primitives),
  `api/path_finding_logic.py` (dependency / bounding-box ordering graph, NetworkX),
  `api/test_main.py` (pytest suite with cached Onshape fixtures), `serial_communicator.py` (USB
  serial bridge), and `arduino/firmware_v3/firmware_v3.ino`
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#file-structure).
- Frontend: a p5.js (WEBGL + 2D) client — `geometry_builder.js`, `tabs.js` (per-bed tabs and the
  printable paper blueprint), `components.js`, `paperControls.js`, `utils.js`, `constants.js`,
  `state.js`, `primitiveSchema.js`, `stackManager.js`, `stackIntegration.js`, `ui.js`, `buttons.js`;
  templates use a bento layout (`index.html`, `header.html`, `settings.html`)
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#file-structure).

### 6.5 Version history

All dates and contents are from the README's roadmap. (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#roadmap)

| Version | Date | What shipped |
|---|---|---|
| Version 1 | 7/17/2025 | Submission to ACM SCF 2025 |
| Version 1.1 | 8/8/2025 | Fixes and new features |
| Version 2.0 | 12/20/2025 | Onshape integration, new features, bug fixes |
| Version 2.1 | 7/8/2026 | Correct extrusion order from Onshape, fewer API calls |
| Version 2.2 | 7/2026 (month only) | Branch junctions, ribbed branches, the printable paper blueprint |

- **Version 1 — 7/17/2025:** submission to ACM SCF 2025
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#roadmap).
- **Version 1.1 — 8/8/2025:** revamped the data structure representing the extrusion; added detection
  of an unsupported segment that is too long; 3D hinge and curve visualization (beta) with fixed
  dimension computations; editing of previous segments; a settings page for kiln dimensions (beta),
  Arduino serial logging, and "ofer mode"; fixed Arduino memory issues; rendered the machine; tested
  the CAD tool with the machine to confirm all primitives work as intended; cleaned up structure and
  documented the code (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#roadmap).
- **Version 2.0 — 12/20/2025:** implemented features and fixed bugs according to user-study feedback;
  full editing of primitives from the Function Stack; selecting a stack item highlights its location
  in the 3D and 2D views; CeraPiper became an in-app Onshape extension, so designs made in Onshape
  open automatically in CeraPiper for fabrication (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#roadmap).
- **Version 2.1 — 7/8/2026:** print order of imported designs now follows the order in which pipes
  were attached in Onshape (decoded from each feature's attachment reference), so branching designs
  print each branch as one contiguous chain; bounding boxes are now used only to position
  perpendicular attachments, with the old bounding-box ordering kept as a fallback; Onshape API
  requests per import greatly reduced, a typical design needing 1 request instead of one per part
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#roadmap).
- **Version 2.2 — 7/2026:** added the interactive paper blueprint (20 cm-wide bed instruction sheet
  with toggleable layers and per-bed part names, numbers, and notes); added an Extrude action that
  compiles the design and prints the bed tabs, plus a per-bed tabs panel in the header; perpendicular
  branch pipes now carve a matching hex hole in the host pipe, with the cut-out marked on the
  blueprint for hand-carving; ribbed profiles supported on perpendicular and branch pipes; fixed
  diameter and connector geometry for perpendicular connections; firmware refinements — corrected
  die-travel depth for rib crest timing and reversed the die direction to match the extrusion
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#roadmap).
- The roadmap gives Version 2.2 a month only, not a day, so any "shipped" date for the paper
  blueprint can only be July 2026 (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#roadmap).

**Next steps listed in the README**

- A calipered test print to reconcile the die-travel reference constants before merging
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#next-steps).
- Tighter integration with the surrounding Onshape environment, reusing native CAD commands
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#next-steps).
- Design the global 3D form first, then apply the primitives onto it — a recurring request from the
  user study (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#next-steps).

### 6.6 Study results, exactly as the README states them

Only the README's own account of the study is publishable. The larger study documented in the paper
draft cannot be cited while the draft is under review — see 6.7 and the TODO(alex) lines in 6.10.

- In a study with eight designers and makers, all eight reported they could focus on their design
  intent without attending to machine-specific detail
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#from-cam-to-cad).
- Seven of eight said the workflow felt like designing in a CAD environment rather than operating a
  machine (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#from-cam-to-cad).
- The die sizes were the constraint participants most often named as helping their design decisions
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#from-cam-to-cad).
- Version 2.0 implemented features and fixed bugs according to feedback from the user study, so the
  study preceded December 2025 in the roadmap's own sequence
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#roadmap). This does not by itself establish that the eight-person
  study and the study behind the draft are the same event — see TODO(alex).

### 6.7 Publication status and the review embargo

**Published (safe to cite):**

- The earlier, machine-facing CAM version of CeraPiper was published at ACM SCF 2025
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#from-cam-to-cad).
- DOI: https://doi.org/10.1145/3745778.3766644
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#from-cam-to-cad).
- Full citation, as given in the CAD draft's reference list: Ofer Berman, Ethan Zhi Ming Seiz, and
  Thijs Roumen. 2025. CeraPiper: Custom Extruded Ceramics for Heat Exchange. In Proceedings of the ACM
  Symposium on Computational Fabrication (SCF '25). ACM, New York, NY, USA, Article 11, 16 pages.
  doi:10.1145/3745778.3766644 (content/source/cerapiper/user-study-paper/text.md#page-16,
  reference [10]).
- Alex is not among those three authors (content/source/cerapiper/user-study-paper/text.md#page-16).

**Under anonymous review (hard rule for every downstream agent and for the site):**

- The paper documenting the CAD layer is a 20-page draft, bylined "Anonymous Author(s)" with
  placeholder ACM conference and DOI boilerplate, and it is under anonymous review
  (content/source/cerapiper/user-study-paper/text.md#page-1, #page-2; content/source/CLAUDE.md —
  cerapiper row; CLAUDE.md — Featured work).
- Therefore: **do not call it published, do not call it accepted, do not quote any sentence from it,
  do not reproduce, crop, or adapt any of its page images, and do not state its study's numbers on
  the site.** Use only `content/source/cerapiper/README.md` for publishable CeraPiper copy
  (content/source/CLAUDE.md — cerapiper row; CLAUDE.md — Featured work).
- `content/source/cerapiper/user-study-paper/` is gitignored and exists only on Alex's machine
  (CLAUDE.md — Source material).
- The résumé's phrasing — that a paper is eventually being written and approved by the SCF research
  conference — must not be reused, because it reads as an expected acceptance
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png; CLAUDE.md — Featured work).
- Safe formulation for the site: the earlier CAM version was published at ACM SCF 2025; work on the
  CAD layer is written up in a paper that is currently under review
  (content/source/cerapiper/README.md#from-cam-to-cad; content/source/CLAUDE.md — cerapiper row).

Content map, smaller pieces section. Every fact below carries its source in parentheses. Sources are
repo-relative paths; `#page-N` / `#P-N` / `#LNNN` point at the page or line inside the file.
`CLAUDE.md` means the root `CLAUDE.md` of this repo. Essay-derived items appear only as neutral
one-clause facts — no essay wording is reproduced anywhere in this file.

Credit convention used throughout: where a source names Alex, his role is stated explicitly; where a
source describes team work, it is written as "the team". Where readers disagree,
`content/source/resume/resume-current.png` wins and the conflict is recorded as a `TODO(alex):` line.

Overlap note: §5.12 of `content/notes/sections/05-robotics.md` covers the same outreach programs from
the robotics team's side, using the Tritonics team documents and their team-level totals (500+
volunteer hours, 10,000+ individuals reached, 12+ FIRST teams). §7.3 below covers the same programs
as Alex's own smaller pieces, using his portfolio page and his résumés as the primary sources. The
two sections must not be merged on the site without resolving the count conflicts flagged in both.

## 7. Smaller pieces

Four items that are smaller than the featured work but are named in root CLAUDE.md as part of the
site's content: Electric Road, the IDEA Club, outreach, and Extended Memory (CLAUDE.md — Smaller
pieces). Three of the four have primary sources in `content/source/`. The fourth, Extended Memory,
has no source anywhere in the repo except root CLAUDE.md itself; see §7.4.

---

### 7.1 Electric Road (individual Mastery project)

**Role:** Individual project. Alex's own portfolio page labels it a Mastery Project and prints
"Individual Project" where the other six pages print a team role
(content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1). The page uses first person for the build: it
states that he built the scaled testing model (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
The résumé lists it under "Tully Tech – Select Inventions", where Alex is described as founder and
innovator of the startup (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).

**Dates:** None. The portfolio page carries no date, season, or year
(content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1). The résumé gives no date for any Tully Tech
invention (content/source/resume/resume-current.png). See the TODO at the end of this subsection.

**One line:** A roadway concept that charges electric vehicles as they drive — a solar-powered road
embedded with wireless charging coils — demonstrated with a scaled testing model
(content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).

#### What the project was

- The stated goal was to develop a roadway capable of charging electric vehicles as they drive
  (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- The final concept was a solar-powered road embedded with wireless charging coils, enabling cars to
  recharge while in motion (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- The résumé describes the same project as a prototype solar-powered road that wirelessly charges
  electric cars while they are in motion (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).
- The résumé frames it as a proof of concept and an experiential project, undertaken to apply physics
  principles and engineering techniques (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).
- Alex's private application drafts list the project among his portfolio media pieces, alongside Ball
  Drive and Swerve Drive (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L3738), and state that his
  school's Mastery program is what enabled both the prosthetic arm and the solar-powered wireless
  charging road (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L5766-L5767).

#### What was built and what it proved

- Alex built a scaled testing model to demonstrate feasibility
  (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- The page states the model proved that solar panels could generate sufficient power to wirelessly
  transfer energy to a moving vehicle, including at high speeds
  (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- The physical model is photographed on the page: a blue mat carrying hand-wound copper coils, a
  small green wireless-charging receiver board, a breadboard with components, and jumper wiring
  (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1, photo at approximately x 605–930, y 205–355
  of the 980×560 page). This is the only photograph of a physical prototype anywhere in the seven
  portfolio pages (content/source/portfolio/01-monti-robot.jpg through
  content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg).
- No measured number of any kind appears on the page: no power figure, no speed, no efficiency, no
  transfer distance, no coil count in text (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- No award, no "Key Innovation(s)" label box, and no "Of Note" line appears on this page, unlike
  pages 1, 3, and 5 (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).

#### Statistics

There are no statistics on this project. Both of its sources are qualitative
(content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1; content/source/resume/resume-current.png). The
only countable details are visual, taken from the renders themselves, and they are described in the
image list below rather than used as claims. The phrase "sufficient power … even at high speeds" is
an unquantified claim with no supporting measurement in any source
(content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1) — see the TODO below before the site repeats it.

#### Images

All three sit on one 980×560 black page; regions are approximate and were read off the page image
(content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg, 980×560 JPEG, verified with PIL).

- Concept CAD render — a grey road segment with yellow dashed centre markings, a purple SUV-shaped
  car, and four tall grey cylindrical roadside pillars, each topped with a tilted solar panel and
  carrying a large disk (the coil) facing the road. Low-fidelity render on black. Approximately
  x 595–915, y 5–200 (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- Photo of the physical scaled test model — blue mat, hand-wound copper coils, green receiver board,
  breadboard, jumper wires. Slightly soft focus. Approximately x 605–930, y 205–355
  (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- White line-art diagram on black — top-down outline of a car with mirrors and four wheels, with a
  multi-ring concentric receiving coil centred beneath it. Approximately x 600–900, y 365–500
  (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- A smaller duplicate of the concept render appears as a résumé thumbnail, approximately
  x 40–150, y 1060–1165 on the 952×1260 page; the portfolio-page copy is larger and should be
  preferred (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).

#### Open questions

- TODO(alex): Project name. The portfolio page and root CLAUDE.md call it "Electric Road"
  (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1; CLAUDE.md), while the résumé calls it
  "ENERGY ROAD: No Pitstops" (content/source/resume/resume-current.png). The repo's tie-break rule
  prefers the current résumé, which would make "Energy Road: No Pitstops" the published name, but
  both the site's own CLAUDE.md and Alex's portfolio page use "Electric Road". Alex to pick one.
- TODO(alex): Any measured results from the scaled model — power generated, transfer distance,
  vehicle speed, efficiency — to support the "sufficient power … even at high speeds" claim, which
  carries no measurement in any source (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- TODO(alex): When was Electric Road built? No source gives a year, and the project's only timing cue
  is that it was a Mastery project (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- TODO(alex): Is the pillar-mounted coil arrangement in the render the real proposed design, or a
  presentation simplification? The render shows roadside pillars, while the companion diagram shows a
  receiving coil under the car and the text describes coils embedded in the road
  (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg#page-1).
- TODO(alex): Does a higher-resolution copy of the concept render or the test-model photo exist? The
  page copies are roughly 320×195 and 325×150 px, too small for a hero image
  (content/source/portfolio/06-electric-road.jpg).
- TODO(alex): Fix the résumé typo if its wording is reused: it reads "while in they are in motion"
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).

### 7.2 IDEA Club (co-founder and lead)

**Role:** Co-founder. The résumé states that Alex co-founded the program
(content/source/resume/resume-current.png). His private application drafts list his title on the
activity as co-founder and lead (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1538-L1540), which
is the source root CLAUDE.md follows in calling him "co-founder and lead" (CLAUDE.md — Smaller
pieces).

**Dates:** The résumé gives none (content/source/resume/resume-current.png). His private drafts place
the activity in grades 9 through 12 at roughly two hours a week over thirty-two weeks a year
(content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1546-L1552).

**One line:** A student-run design and engineering program Alex co-founded that trains students in
3D CAD and printing, design thinking, rapid prototyping, fabrication, and shop safety
(content/source/resume/resume-current.png).

#### Name

- The résumé sidebar heads the entry "INNOVATIVE DESIGN & ENGINEERING"
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).
- The older résumé's community section calls the same activity "IDEA design & build workshops"
  (content/source/resume/resume-older.png).
- Root CLAUDE.md calls it the "IDEA Club" (CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces).
- Alex's private drafts give the expansion as Innovative Design, Engineering & Automotive
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1538-L1542). This is the only place in the repo
  where the "A" is expanded; neither résumé spells out the acronym
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png; content/source/resume/resume-older.png).

#### What it trains

- The résumé lists five subjects: 3D CAD/printing, design thinking, rapid prototyping, fabrication,
  and shop safety (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).
- Root CLAUDE.md compresses the same list to CAD, CNC, and fabrication (CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces).
  CNC appears in Alex's private drafts' version of the subject list but not on either résumé
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1543-L1544;
  content/source/resume/resume-current.png).
- His private drafts add that he designed and led rapid prototyping, laser cutting, and design
  thinking projects, and that the training covered makerspace safety
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1543-L1546).

#### Statistics

- **70+ students trained.** Source: the résumé sidebar, in both versions
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png; content/source/resume/resume-older.png). No external
  citation is given in the source. Corroborated in Alex's private drafts
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1543-L1544) and repeated in root CLAUDE.md
  (CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces). This is the figure to publish, because the current résumé is the
  repo's tie-break source.
- **40+ students reached through IDEA design & build workshops.** Source: the older résumé's
  "Community Engagement (Quantifiable Impact)" section
  (content/source/resume/resume-older.png). No external citation is given in the source. This number
  sits on the same page as that résumé's own "70+ students" sidebar claim
  (content/source/resume/resume-older.png), so the two are either different measures — trained versus
  reached at workshops — or one is stale. See the TODO below.
- **Roughly 2 hours a week, 32 weeks a year, grades 9–12.** Source: Alex's private drafts
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1546-L1552). No external citation. Confirmation
  only; not for publication as a statistic without Alex's approval.

#### Images

- None. The IDEA Club has no photograph, logo, or render anywhere in `content/source/`. Verified: no
  portfolio page covers it (content/source/portfolio/01-monti-robot.jpg through
  content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg), and the résumé entry is text only with no
  thumbnail (content/source/resume/resume-current.png). If the site gives IDEA a card, it needs a new
  photo from Alex — see the TODO below.

#### Open questions

- TODO(alex): Reconcile "70+ students" trained (content/source/resume/resume-current.png) with "40+
  students reached through IDEA design & build workshops"
  (content/source/resume/resume-older.png). Are these two different counts, or is one out of date?
- TODO(alex): Confirm the club's public name and the expansion of the acronym. Three variants exist:

  content/source/resume/resume-older.png).
- TODO(alex): Founding year, who the co-founder was, and whether the club is still running

- TODO(alex): Any photo of an IDEA session, a student project, or the makerspace that can be

### 7.3 Outreach

**Role:** Mixed, and it matters. On his own portfolio page Alex uses first person for the Hour of
Code curriculum and the e-waste drive, and describes running the Hudson Guild programs
(content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1). On the Tritonics team documents the same
outreach programs are presented as team work, and one of them — the micro-funding website — is
credited by name to a teammate (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-e.jpg). The
team's roster card for Alex describes his role as managing team strategy, the electrical system, and
operations, and records that he is proudest of team-building, recruiting and training rookies
(content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-e.jpg). Per-program credit is spelled out
below.

**Dates:** No outreach source in the repo carries a calendar year
(content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1;
content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png). Alex's private drafts put the
outreach work in grades 9 through 12 (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1564-L1577).

---

#### 7.3.1 Hudson Guild — weekly robotics programs

- Alex's portfolio page states that he ran programs weekly, introducing younger students to robotics
  and other basic STEM concepts (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1).
- This is the only source in the repo that describes a Hudson Guild program. In the Tritonics team
  documents Hudson Guild appears only in a list of nonprofit partners, alongside FreeCycles, with no
  program detail (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-8).
- Alex's private drafts add, as neutral facts, that Hudson Guild serves lower-income students and
  that the robotics class ran after school once or twice a week
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L4371-L4372).

**Statistics.** The only quantity given is the frequency, "weekly"
(content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1). No source gives a student count, a session
count, or a date range for Hudson Guild.

**Images.** One photo, on the portfolio page: several young children seated on a classroom floor
around a tablet with a blue screen, wooden chairs and white tables behind them; rounded corners,
approximately x 65–300, y 310–490 of the 980×560 page
(content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1). The children's faces are visible and they
are minors — see the clearance TODO in §7.3.7.

---

#### 7.3.2 Hour of Code workshops

- Alex's portfolio page states that he organised and ran over 20 workshops and designed a curriculum
  for lower-grade students introducing coding and logical thinking skills
  (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1).
- The Tritonics team documents describe Hour of Code as a K–8 program the team leads in schools and
  community centres as a low-barrier introduction to computational thinking and robotics
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png#P7).
- Alex's private drafts add, as neutral facts, that the sessions were run with students in grades 1
  through 4 (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L62-L64).

**Statistics.** Four different counts exist for the same program, and they do not agree:

- **20+ workshops.** Source: Alex's own portfolio page
  (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1). No external citation in the source. This
  is the figure root CLAUDE.md uses (CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces) and the one closest to a
  first-person source, since the résumé does not mention Hour of Code at all
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png).
- **30+ sessions.** Source: the Tritonics Reach planning slide and the Reach poster, describing the
  team's programme (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png#P7). No external
  citation in the source.
- **40+ Hour of Code sessions led.** Source: Alex's private drafts, activity list
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1573). Confirmation only; not for publication.
- **13 one-hour sessions.** Source: an earlier note in the same private file
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L62-L64). Confirmation only; not for publication.

Team-level downstream figures also exist and belong to the team, not to Alex: 250+ students
introduced to FIRST concepts, 80+ transitioned into FIRST programs, and 60+ who later joined FTC or
FLL teams (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png#P7;
content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-5). These are already recorded in §5.12 of
`content/notes/sections/05-robotics.md` and must not be re-attributed to Alex personally.

**Images.** One photo, on the portfolio page: Alex standing in a white dress shirt and black trousers
holding a dark object, with young students at a black lab table and on a stool, and a black robot mat
with white circles on the floor at lower right; approximately x 380–600, y 310–495
(content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1). A second, unrelated Hour of Code classroom
photo appears on the team's Reach slide, showing children at laptops with an instructor
(content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png#P7). Both show minors — see §7.3.7.

#### 7.3.3 School e-waste drive

- Alex's portfolio page states that he organised an in-school e-waste drive collecting 40+ pounds of
  e-waste, which was then either donated or properly recycled
  (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1).
- The same page states, in the present tense at the time it was made, that he was working towards
  expanding it into a city-wide effort, setting up dozens of collection points and promotion with
  help from the NYC department of sanitation
  (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1).
- The e-waste drive appears nowhere in the Tritonics team documents — not in the 30-page poster deck,
  the championship slides, or the posters (verified by keyword search across
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md; the remaining robotics files are images and
  are listed in §7.7).
- Alex's private drafts add, as neutral facts, that the drive began locally and involved advertising,
  sorting, cleaning, donating, and recycling, with a cross-school competition in progress
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L64-L67), and that the second-year expansion
  covered 200+ organisations in partnership with NYC Sanitation, local officials, and media
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1586-L1598).

**Statistics.**

- **40+ pounds of e-waste collected.** Source: Alex's portfolio page
  (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1). No external citation in the source. This
  is the figure root CLAUDE.md carries (CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces).
- **Dozens of collection points, city-wide.** Source: the same page, stated as a plan rather than an
  achievement (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1). No external citation. The
  site must present this as planned, not done, unless Alex confirms otherwise.
- **200+ organisations in the year-two expansion.** Source: Alex's private drafts
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L1594-L1598). Confirmation only; not for
  publication, and a different unit from the 40+ lbs figure, so the two must not be blended.

**Images.** Three items on the portfolio page (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1):

- Photo of Alex smiling, crouching beside black e-waste collection bins, framed photographs on the
  wall behind him; black t-shirt, grey trousers; portrait orientation, approximately x 800–910,
  y 255–490. This is the only clear photograph of Alex in the seven portfolio pages, and the only
  candidate portrait in this section's sources. Source width is roughly 110 px, so it is
  thumbnail-scale only (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1).
- Two stacked document thumbnails, approximately x 680–790, y 255–490: a white page of bulleted text
  that is illegible at source resolution, and a green card headed "Computers + Accesories" with an
  illustration — apparently an e-waste category poster. Not usable on the site; text illegible, and
  the card contains a spelling error that must not be reproduced
  (content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1).

#### 7.3.5 Micro-funding platform

**Credit — read before writing copy.** Root CLAUDE.md lists the micro-funding platform among Alex's
smaller pieces (CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces), but the team's own roster slide credits the Micro
Funding website to a teammate, Miland A, Code Specialist (Y2), under his "Proud of" line
(content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-e.jpg). The same slide's card for Alex
names team strategy, the electrical system, and operations as his role, and team-building as what he
is proudest of (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-e.jpg). No source in the
repo says Alex built the site. The safe framing is that the platform is his team's, that he led the
team that launched it, and that the fundraising it supported is described in his own drafts as work
he took part in — see the TODO in §7.3.7.

- The team documents describe the platform as a student-designed micro-funding site that lets
  robotics teams create campaigns and receive community support, to fund programs, start teams, or
  contribute to a shared robotics fund
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-5).
- An older description in the same deck frames it as a MicroFunding effort to raise money for a local
  robotics team by asking FTC and FRC teams for small donations, so that collective contributions
  support robotics education, equipment, and learning opportunities
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-25).
- The deck lists "MicroFunding support to FRC + FTC" among the teams the Tritonics directly mentored
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-8).
- The site is branded "FTC Microfunding" with the tagline "Connecting teams and sponsors alike", and
  its address is https://microfunding.tritonics.org/
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/pages/page-05.jpg;
  content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-b.png#P8).
- The team launched it in response to a 50% mid-season budget cut, alongside budget tracking and the
  use of sustainable materials (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-3).

**Who it helped.**

- Root CLAUDE.md states that the platform helped robotics teams in Ukraine, Ghana, and the Blackfeet
  Reservation (CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces).
- Alex's private drafts corroborate all three, describing a micro-funding platform that connects
  robotics teams with their local communities and naming teams in Ukraine, Ghana, and Montana's
  Blackfeet Reservation as the ones helped to raise funds
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L277-L279).
- The team documents corroborate Ghana and the Blackfoot community but not Ukraine: the Reach
  planning slide lists "Ghana FTC (monthly)" and "Blackfoot Reserve FLL Teams (monthly +
  fundraising)" under sustained engagement
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-d.png#P7), and the deck records monthly
  FTC workshops at Accra High School in Ghana and a collaboration with Blackfoot 8th graders
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-29). Ukraine appears nowhere in the
  Tritonics documents (verified by keyword search across
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md).
- The deck also builds the reservation programme as an FLL programme, spelled "Blackfleet"
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-8), while page 29 of the same deck spells
  it "Blackfoot" (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-29) and root CLAUDE.md
  spells it "Blackfeet" (CLAUDE.md). See the TODO in §7.3.7.

**Statistics.**

- **$1,000 raised for a Ukraine robotics team.** Source: the older résumé's Community Engagement
  section (content/source/resume/resume-older.png). No external citation in the source. This is the
  résumé figure and therefore the one to publish. See §7.3.6.
- **$2k raised to send a Ukrainian team to the World Championship.** Source: Alex's private drafts
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L68-L69). Confirmation only; not for publication,
  and it conflicts with the résumé's $1,000 — see the TODO in §7.3.7.
- No source gives an amount raised for the Ghana or Blackfeet/Blackfoot teams
  (content/source/resume/resume-older.png; content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md#page-29).

**Images.**

- A clean QR code for the platform and a screenshot of the site's hero section appear on the deck's
  Reach page — QR at approximately x 20–210, y 575–775 and screenshot at x 230–430, y 575–735
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/pages/page-05.jpg). The QR should not be reused; link
  the URL instead.
- A smaller copy of the same site screenshot appears on the team planning slide
  (content/source/robotics/championships-presentation/slide-f.jpg) and on the Sustain poster
  (content/source/robotics/posters/poster-a.png). All are small; a fresh screenshot of the live site
  would be better if the site is still up — see the TODO in §7.3.7.

#### 7.3.6 The older résumé's community numbers

These four lines appear only in the "COMMUNITY ENGAGEMENT (Quantifiable Impact)" section of the older
résumé, which the current résumé drops in order to make room for the CeraPiper entry
(content/source/resume/resume-older.png; content/source/resume/resume-current.png). Everything else
on the two pages is otherwise identical (content/source/resume/resume-older.png;
content/source/resume/resume-current.png). None of the four carries an external citation in the
source.

- **$1,000 raised for a Ukraine robotics team.** Source: content/source/resume/resume-older.png. No
  external citation. Related to the micro-funding work in §7.3.5; conflicts with the $2k figure in
  Alex's private drafts (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L68-L69).
- **40+ students reached through IDEA design & build workshops.** Source:
  content/source/resume/resume-older.png. No external citation. Conflicts with the "70+ students"
  claim in the sidebar of the same page — see §7.2.
- **1,000+ food-insecure people nourished at a food pantry.** Source:
  content/source/resume/resume-older.png. No external citation. This is the only place in the repo
  where food pantry service is claimed as an activity: it is absent from the current résumé, from the
  portfolio pages, from the Tritonics documents, and from root CLAUDE.md
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png;
  content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg#page-1;
  content/source/robotics/posters/poster-deck/text.md; CLAUDE.md). A food pantry is mentioned twice in
  Alex's private drafts, but in a different context — as one of three competing origin stories for the
  prosthetic arm, not as volunteering
  (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt#L6366; #L6389; see §4 of
  content/notes/sections/04-prosthetic.md, which records that conflict). The two must not be
  conflated. See the TODO in §7.3.7.
- **80+ children inspired to explore STEM through coding & robotics.** Source:
  content/source/resume/resume-older.png. No external citation. This is a count of children reached,
  and must not be confused with root CLAUDE.md's separate "80+ member club" figure, which describes
  the size of the school robotics club (CLAUDE.md — Featured work). The two numbers count different
  things.

The current résumé retains none of these four lines (content/source/resume/resume-current.png), so
publishing them means publishing a number Alex has since taken off his résumé. See the TODO in
§7.3.7.

### 7.4 Extended Memory (concept)

**Role:** Unknown. No source states a role, because no source describes the project at all beyond
root CLAUDE.md's one entry (CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces).

**Dates:** None (CLAUDE.md).

**One line, and the whole of what is known:** Extended Memory is a concept for a wearable second
memory — for example glasses or a hat — that recalls names, faces, and moments only when needed
(CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces).

#### Source status

- Root CLAUDE.md is the only source. It lists Extended Memory under "Smaller pieces" and labels it a
  concept (CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces).
- The term appears nowhere else in the repository. A whole-tree keyword search for "extended memory",
  "second memory", "recalls names", and "remember names" across every `.md` and `.txt` file returns
  only the two lines in root CLAUDE.md and its own open-questions list (CLAUDE.md, lines 99–100 and
  125).
- It is absent from both résumés, so it is not among the Tully Tech select inventions, which are
  listed as the prosthetic arm, AntiCam, and Energy Road
  (content/source/resume/resume-current.png; content/source/resume/resume-older.png).
- It is absent from Alex's seven portfolio pages (content/source/portfolio/01-monti-robot.jpg through
  content/source/portfolio/07-community-events.jpg).
- It is absent from the private application drafts, which otherwise catalogue his projects and
  ventures in detail (content/source/essays/college-essays-private.txt, whole-file keyword search).
- Root CLAUDE.md itself flags the item as unresolved, listing "whether to include Extended Memory and
  outreach on the home page or only on /about" among its open questions for Alex (CLAUDE.md — Open
  questions).

#### Statistics

None. There is no number attached to this item in any source (CLAUDE.md).

#### Images

None. There is no render, sketch, photograph, or diagram of Extended Memory anywhere in
`content/source/` (whole-tree search).

#### What the site can say

Nothing beyond the single sentence above, and only if Alex confirms it. Because the concept has no
build, no prototype, and no primary source, the honest presentation is either a one-line "what's
next" note, clearly marked as a concept, or omission. It must not be written up like the other
smaller pieces, which all have at least one primary document behind them.

#### Open questions

- TODO(alex): Does Extended Memory exist beyond the idea — any sketch, CAD, prototype, or notes? Root
  CLAUDE.md is the only source in the repo (CLAUDE.md — Smaller pieces).
- TODO(alex): Which form factor is the real one — glasses, a hat, or something else? CLAUDE.md names
  both glasses and a hat as examples (CLAUDE.md).
- TODO(alex): Is "Extended Memory" the public name, or a working title (CLAUDE.md)?
- TODO(alex): Should Extended Memory appear on the site at all, and if so where? Root CLAUDE.md
  already raises this as an open question (CLAUDE.md — Open questions).
- TODO(alex): If it does appear, how should the privacy angle be handled given that a wearable which
  records faces and moments is the inverse of AntiCam's premise, and AntiCam is framed as giving
  people control over when they are seen (content/source/resume/resume-current.png)?

repo-relative paths; `#page-N` / `#LNNN` point at the page or line inside the file. `CLAUDE.md`
